市場焦點
美國
• 9月綜合PMI升至58.4，創2021年7月以來新高 ，製造業與服務業同步加速 ，新訂單及就業亦
明顯成長 ，顯示消費 、AI投資與國防支出支撐景氣動能 。不過，需求快速擴張也加劇供應鏈
瓶頸，企業投入價格升至 2022年10月以來高點，燃料、運輸及原物料成本上升 ，顯示景氣韌
性與通膨壓力同步升溫，增加Fed政策維持緊縮的壓力。
美國
期中選舉
• 目前民調顯示 ，民主黨可能取得兩院或眾議院多數 ，這將有助於縮減未來財政赤字預期 ，進
而帶動10年期美債殖利率下滑 ，對長天期美債形成支撐 。此外既有資料顯示 ，選後一年美股
平均報酬率達12.4%，主因為政治不確定性降低，市場焦點重新回到經濟成長、企業獲利及貨
幣政策等基本面因素，進而帶動投資信心提升。

越南
• 越南股市正式納入富時羅素新興市場指數 。估計此次納入最高可引導 60億美元的資金重新流
入越南。資產管理公司Vanguard也計畫在未來幾年內，將對越南的投資額提升至約 25億美元。
• 越南股市短期仍面臨逆風 ，除了能源通膨壓力居高不下恐拖累經濟成長 ，今年以來越南成為
美國最大貿易逆差國，加上美方企圖阻止越南成為中國商品流入的中轉國 ，美對越的301條款
調查為越南出口額帶來更多不確定性。

美國景氣強勢擴張，通膨與利率壓力再升
• 企業活動創逾五年新高 ，製造業與服務業同步加速擴張 ：S&P
Global美國9月綜合PMI初值升至58.4，創2021年7月以來新高，
且連續四個月加速成長；服務業 PMI升至58.7（59個月高點）、
製造業PMI升至57.0（52個月高點），產出亦同步加速 ，加上
訂單與就業成長皆達近年最快 ，顯示內需、AI投資及國防支出
共同支撐企業活動。S&P Global依PMI調查推估經濟年成長約
5%，Q3則上看4%，反映短期景氣動能明顯增強。
• 供給受限推高成本 ，通膨壓力再升溫： 需求快速擴張同時推高
供應鏈與產能壓力 ，企業未完成訂單升至 2022年5月以來高點 ，
供應商交貨延遲亦明顯惡化 ，反映產能不足 ，投入價格指數升

至2022年10月以來新高 ，企業主要反映燃料 、運輸、原物料
及薪資成本上升 ，顯示供給受限與能源成本可能持續向終端價
格傳導，增加通膨黏著風險並提高Fed維持緊縮政策的壓力。
• 後市觀察：數據公布後，10年期美債殖利率一度升至約 5.12%，
創2007年以來新高 ，市場對10月再升息的押注亦升溫 ，短線
需關注供應鏈壓力是否緩解 ，以及後續ISM、就業與物價數據
能否確認通膨再升溫趨勢 。投資策略建議關注利率敏感度較低
的高品質成長股或防禦型資產 ，可適度布局受惠強勁內需與 AI
／國防支出的美股大型科技與工業類股 ，並留意升息環境下金
融股的利差受益機會 ，整體建議維持分散配置並嚴控利率風險 。
資料來源：S&P Global PMI, Bureau of Economic Analysis via S&P Global Market
Intelligence， 2026.9.24

能源成本升高推升通膨，利率維持高檔壓力升溫
• 能源與運輸成本上升，增加通膨黏著風險：中東衝突與
俄羅斯煉油設施遭攻擊 ，使全球柴油供應持續偏緊 ，燃
料價格及運輸成本同步上升；由於柴油廣泛應用於運輸 、
農業與製造業，價格上漲將透過物流及生產成本向下游
傳導。目前全球柴油庫存偏低 、煉油產能受限，若供應
中斷延續，可能使能源價格維持高檔並延長通膨壓力。
• 公債與企業債供給增加 ，長端利率壓力升高：美國財政
赤字及政府融資需求偏高 ，加上大型科技公司持續為 AI
資料中心等基礎建設籌資 ，債券供給增加使市場承接壓
力升高；在通膨預期未完全穩定的情況下 ，投資人對長
天期債券要求較高的期限溢酬 ，進一步推升長端殖利率 ，
同時AI相關企業債近期亦出現利差擴大 ，反映市場對大

量發債及資本支出回收期的關注升高。
• 資產配置面臨成長與通膨雙重拉扯： 能源價格、企業融
資成本及長端殖利率若持續處於高檔 ，將提高企業資金
成本並壓縮高估值資產的評價空間；同時 ，利率與能源
價格波動也可能擴大股 、債市震盪。因此資產配置建議
降低長天期債券對利率變化的敏感度 ，提高對通膨與利
率波動的防禦能力 ，並以現金流穩定、具備定價能力之
優質標的為核心。
資料來源：Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis via FRED ， 2026.9.8
美國零售柴油價格與通膨預期走勢對比
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.9

美國期中選舉-目前民調顯示民主黨可能取得兩院或眾議院多數
• 目前民調顯示民主黨可能取得兩院或眾議院多數 ：最新民調顯示，民主黨
於11月美國期中選舉，取得國會多數席次的機率已領先共和黨，根據市場
情境分析，期中選舉不影響既有法案執行 ，但將左右未來兩年的財政預算 、
稅制改革 、政府支出及國會監督權限 。情境(1)若共和黨續掌國會 ，減稅
政策延長、產業回流、關稅及移民限制等議程有望加速推進 ，財政擴張力
道可能進一步增強 。情境(2)分裂國會 ，兩黨制衡將使重大法案推動難度
提高，新增減稅與大型財政刺激方案較難落地 。情境(3)民主黨全面掌控
國會，則可能提高對政府政策的監督力度 ，政策方向偏向提高稅負與監管 ，
壓抑企業獲利 ，使政策環境趨向平衡。

顧
美國期中選舉-對於股債市的影響
• 對股市的影響：美國銀行分析1900至2025年共31次美國期中選舉，資料發現標普500在期中選舉前12個月平均報酬約為2.9%，
低於所有年度平均 8.9%。因為市場在選舉前往往受到政策不確定性 、政黨競爭及投資人觀望情緒影響 ，波動相對較大 。然而
此現象不代表選舉必然導致市場疲弱，因為當時的經濟環境、通膨、利率及企業獲利等因素同樣扮演重要角色。期中選舉後12
個月的股市表現通常較佳 。歷史數據顯示，選後一年美股平均報酬率達12.4%。主因為政治不確定性降低，市場焦點重新回到
經濟成長、企業獲利及貨幣政策等基本面因素，進而帶動投資信心提升。
• 對債市的影響：若民主黨取得國會主導權，市場普遍預期財政支出擴張幅度將較受節制 ，有助於縮減未來財政赤字預期，進而

帶動10年期美債殖利率下滑，對長天期美債形成支撐，若共和黨持續全面掌控國會，市場可能預期減稅及財政擴張政策延續，
進一步推升財政赤字預估，帶動公債殖利率走高，對長天期美債形成壓力，不過市場已部分反映相關預期，實際衝擊幅度有限。
美國期中選舉前後對於美股的影響
資料來源：美國銀行，2026.7
資料來源：復華投信，2026.9

AI建設熱潮吞噬自由現金流
• 資本支出創新高：雲端服務巨頭(包括亞馬遜、Alphabet、微軟、Meta、甲骨文以及SpaceX等)正在全力擴充AI算力與資料
中心，2026年大型雲端服務巨頭的資本支出合計預估將接近 9,600億美元，而2025至2031年的累計AI資本支出更可能逼近
9兆美元。
• 自由現金流出現赤字： 高額的投資加上股東回報(股息與庫藏股買回)，使許多巨頭的自由現金流(FCF)轉為負值(如Alphabet、
亞馬遜、微軟、Meta及甲骨文等)，預計到2027年，部分企業將更加依賴外部融資，而非單靠內部現金生成。
• 債券發行量大增： 為了填補資金缺口 ，科技業債券發行規模急遽上升 ，2026年迄今，各幣別的投資級科技債券發行量已突
破4,000億美元，創下歷史新高。

科技產業AI資本支出規模最大企業
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.23

獲利韌性與金庫深度：強勁EBITDA成為信用安全網
• 上兆美元的流動性護城河： 儘管債務增加，但科技十二巨頭合計持有近 1兆美元的等值現金，整體科技板塊流動性充裕 ，短

• 小結與投資建議：科技巨頭為維持AI技術與基礎設施領先優勢，短期內，負自由現金流將成為常態。然而，這並非財務危機

訊號，而是企業利用超強獲利能力 (高EBITDA)與龐大的現金儲備進行戰略性擴張 ，科技板塊的信用風險目前處於可控且健
康的狀態，投資建議聚焦AI變現能力與EBITDA成長，優先佈局具有強大獲利護城河且能將算力轉化為實質營收的龍頭企業 。

越南股市正式納入富時羅素新興市場指數
• 預估引導60億美元資金流入越南： 富時羅素估計，此次納入最高可引導 60億美元的資金重新流入越南 。受此提振，資產管
理公司Vanguard也計畫在未來幾年內，將對越南的投資額提升至約25億美元。整個指數過渡將分為四個階段推行至2027年：
今年9月率先納入10%，明年3月追加20%，最後於明年6月與9月分別完成剩餘各35%的分配。市場分析指出，隨著明年3月
配額加大，市場關注度與對本地投資人的影響將更為顯著。
• 越南股市短期仍面臨逆風： 儘管官方早前誓言今年 GDP成長目標達10%，但中東衝突以來能源通膨壓力居高不下恐拖累經
濟成長。此外，越南與美國的貿易協商仍在進行中，由於今年以來越南成為美國最大貿易逆差國，加上美方企圖阻止越南成

為中國商品流入的中轉國，美對越的301條款調查為越南出口額帶來更多不確定性。
• 觀點：儘管短線面臨不確定性 ，但越南股市同時具備多項利多題材 ，包含企業獲利成長、公共投資加速、FDI回流及資本市
場改革等。中長期結構仍偏正向，可以衛星配置的角度布局，或是以定期定額的方式參與長期成長潛力。

息日前一日之淨值×100%。

績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。

## Document 076: 261005_ml_TSMC

### Page 1

Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co.

TSMC-Musk: customer relationship intact; 
tech/control sharing with Terafab unlikely

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 3,100 TWD | Price: 2,500 TWD

Customer neutrality central to TSMC’s foundry model 
Elon Musk on Saturday October 3rd responded “Just discussions, but something may come 
of it” to an article discussing the potential manufacturing partnership between TSMC 
and Terafab. From chipset design and foundry service perspective, we expect a deeper 
collaboration as Tesla and SpaceX grow at TSMC (2-3% of its sales) directly with their 
auto/robotics and cloud AI ASICs and indirectly through merchant HPC chipset buys (e.g. 
GPUs, networking switches) based on our estimate. However, the likelihood of TSMC 
licensing technology or operating a fab with Terafab holding a meaningful share should 
be low. Specifically, we would note TSMC’s JV fab record is limited including WaferTech, 
SSMC, VIS, JASM, ESMC and AVSM mostly on mature nodes. The company did not co-
own a fab with Apple who contributed ~21% of its revenue from ’15-25 mostly on the 
advanced processes as it keeps full business control and preserves customer neutrality. 
Terafab capex insufficient for 1 terawatt capacity target 
Based on Terafab’s applications under Texas’s JETI program, it outlined ~US$119bn of 
planned investment across 4 phases from ’26-36, with ~60% of it on tool procurement. 
The spending in ’26-28 would be highly skewed toward facility construction before it 
starts to pull in US$6-8bn equipment per annum from ‘29-36. Based on our estimate of 
US$600mn+ for every 1k WPM expansion on the advanced nodes in the US, Terafab 
could only grow its capacity installed base by ~10k WPM per annum at maximum even if 
it can successfully deliver the technology roadmap through its own R&D effort and Intel 
partnership. This would prevent Terafab’s operation from the economies of scale as the 
node migration continues, keeping TSMC as the major foundry partner for Tesla/SpaceX. 
Stock at mid-cycle supported by robust AI demand pipeline 
We keep our Buy rating on TSMC with PO at NT$3,100 on 20x '27 P/E (vs. current 16x).

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
TSMC was founded in 1987 and is listed in Taiwan 
(2330TT) and the US (TSM/NYS).TSMC is the largest and 
global leader in integrated circuit (IC) manufacturing. As a 
build-to-order foundry, it provides a wide range of value-
add activities: IC manufacturing, mask-making, IC design 
services, turnkey solutions, and process development. We 
attribute its success to its proven, winning business model, 
unparalleled scale advantage, optimized execution, and 
technology scope and depth. 5 shares = 1 ADR.

Investment Rationale 
The ongoing semi content growth in mobile, rise of artificial 
intelligence (AI), and proliferation of Internet of Things (IoT) 
should result in sustainable upside in aggregate computing 
power globally. TSMC, as the leading contract manufacturer 
of semiconductor chips, is in a good position to capitalize. 
We are structurally positive on TSMC and expect it to 
sustain over 15% growth with rising FCF from 2026 
onwards.

### Page 4

Price objective basis & risk 
Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMWF / TSM) 
Our PO for TSMC is NT$3,100 per share (US$590 per ADR), based on 20x 2027E P/E. 
TSMC's valuation has traded in the range of 10-26x PE from 2017. 20x is in the mid to 
high end of the historical range, above the average P/E of 18x. We think a PE above the 
average is justified given TSMC's enhanced industry position and mid-40% 5-year CAGR 
in AI should underpin a rerating

Downside risks to our price objective are: (1) greater-than-expected slowdown in global 
smartphone/consumer electronics demand, (2) Intel's potential insourcing strategy and 
ambitions in foundry service, (3) execution risks on advanced nodes, and (4) higher than 
expected tariffs.

Chroma ATE (2360.TW)
06 October 2026 Citi Research
3
Chroma ATE
Valuation
Our target price of NT$2,500 is based on 40x 2027E P/E, at close to 1x PEG. Chroma shares have re-rated above its historical
range as its growth outlook is now strongly tied to AI infrastructure build out. We estimate 60%+ of its revenue is now AI-
related, with strong growth across product segments including power testers, SLT, metrology, and photonics. Chroma has a
commanding market share in most of these segments and should be well positioned to capitalize on the global AI expansion.

Risks
Downside risks to achieving our target price include: 1) softer AI/CPO growth expectation due to changes in capex outlook or
production bottleneck; 2) fewer SLT needed as a result of shortened cycle time as GPU yield rate improves; 3) disappointing
metrology shipment due to changes at ramp-up schedule; 4) testing process change leading to loss in market share; 5) FX risks
(50-60% of Chroma’s sales are in US$, 10-20% in Rmb).
Prepared for Kevin Lu

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

overall client relationship with the Firm and legal and regulatory constraints.

## Document 116: 9月金融数据点评：关注外部环境对适度宽松货币政策的影响

Source PDF: all report pdf/9月金融数据点评：关注外部环境对适度宽松货币政策的影响.pdf
Report source: 中银证券
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

宏观经济 | 证券研究报告 — 总量点评 2026 年 10 月 15 日

相关研究报告 
《上下游物价指标分歧加大》20261014 
《9 月通胀点评》20261014 
《前三季度进出口数据点评》20261014

中银国际证券股份有限公司 
具备证券投资咨询业务资格 
宏观经济

证券分析师：张晓娇 
xiaojiao.zhang@bocichina.com 
证券投资咨询业务证书编号：S1300514010002

证券分析师：朱启兵 
(8610)66229359 
Qibing.Zhu@bocichina.com 
证券投资咨询业务证书编号：S1300516090001

9 月金融数据点评

关注外部环境对适度宽松货币政策的影响   
9 月新增社融和新增信贷均好于万得一致预期；企业融资需求分层，信用度
较高的企业以企业债为代表的融资需求下降，依赖贷款渠道的企业贷款 9 月
表现相对更好；居民部门加杠杆意愿不强；受资本市场波动影响，存款搬家
有变化的趋势；关注海外加息周期和高利率环境对国内金融条件和货币政策
的潜在影响。 
 9 月新增社融 3.55 万亿元，较去年同期多增 192 亿元，较 8 月多增 1.89 万亿元，
高于万得一致预期的 3.13 万亿元。9 月社融存量同比增长 7.1%，较 8 月下降了
0.07 个百分点，基本持平于万得一致预期的 7.06%。9 月社融口径新增人民币贷
款 1.27 万亿元，较去年同期少增 3342 亿元，较 8 月多增 1.22 万亿元。 
 9 月新增社融较去年同期小幅多增，主要原因是政府债券较去年同期多增了 3438
亿元，同时委托贷款和外币贷款也较去年同期有所多增；从当月新增规模看，新
增融资规模较大的项目，主要是政府债券、人民币贷款和汇票，当月少增的是信
托贷款和企业债。 值得关注的是9 月股票融资表现依然较好， 当月新增658 亿元，
较去年同期也有小幅上升， 但企业债少增425 亿元， 表明企业融资需求依然不强。 
 从社融存量结构来看，2026 年 9 月与 8 月相比，占比上升的有政府债券（上升
0.17 个百分点）和汇票（上升 0.04 个百分点），占比下降的有股票融资（下降
0.01 个百分点）、信托贷款（下降 0.02 个百分点）、企业债（下降 0.08 个百分
点）和人民币贷款（下降 0.16 个百分点）。与去年同期相比，9 月占比上升较多
的是政府债券（上升 1.30 个百分点）和企业债（上升 0.16 个百分点），占比下
降较多的是人民币贷款 （下降1.31 个百分点） 和委托贷款 （下降0.13 个百分点） 。
虽然新增企业债融资规模少增，但企业债融资占比仍有上升趋势。 
 9 月 M2 同比增长 7.2%，较 8 月下降 0.3 个百分点；M1 同比增长 3.2%，较 8 月
下降 0.9 个百分点；M0 同比增长 10.6%，较 8 月下降 0.6 个百分点。9 月活期存
款（M1-M0）环比上升 0.79%，定期存款（M2-M1）环比上升 0.76%，与去年同
期相比，活期存款环比增速偏低，定期存款环比增速偏高，关注资本市场指数波
动对存款变化趋势的影响。 
 9 月关注非银存款回流。9 月金融机构新增存款 1.77 万亿元，多增的主要是居民
存款 2.40 万亿元和企业存款 1.31 万亿元，财政存款和非银存款均少增；与去年
同期相比，9 月新增存款少增 4400 亿元，其中非银存款少增 8500 亿元，居民存
款少增 5600 亿元。9 月新增存款不弱， 但与去年同期相较不强， 其中值得关注的
细节较多：一是企业存款多增，一定程度上印证了企业融资需求不强的原因，二
是居民存款较去年同期少增，关注居民收入增速变化情况，三是财政存款较去年
同期多增， 关注财政支出是否偏慢， 以及对固投增速的影响， 四是非银存款偏弱，
可能代表投资者对资本市场的态度短期偏谨慎。 
 9 月实体经济融资需求延续偏弱。9 月金融机构口径新增人民币贷款 1.08 万亿元，
略高于万得一致预期的 9880 亿元。 其中， 新增短贷及票据3200 亿元， 中长贷9774
亿元， 企业贷1.29 万亿元。 我们认为有三处值得关注， 一是虽然新增贷款超预期，
但是较去年同期少增，主要是居民贷款少增较多，二是新增企业贷款较多，与企
业债发行融资需求偏弱有所不同，三是居民短贷和企业短贷均较去年同期少增较
多。我们认为贷款数据指向了居民部门加杠杆需求不强，以及企业部门内部出现
分化，其中信用等级较高的企业融资需求不强，但依靠信贷的企业中长期贷款需
求仍在。 
 关注外部环境对适度宽松货币政策的影响。9 月底以来，央行货币政策出台了三
项主要调整，一是央行等八部门联合印发《关于金融支持服务业扩能提质的指导
意见》，二是 9 月 29 日发布调整完善若干货币政策工具，包括下调抵押补充贷
款（PSL）利率 0.25 个百分点，将“六张网”建设纳入抵押补充贷款支持领域，及
再贷款扩容等，三是强化财政金融政策协同，实施居民购房贷款贴息政策。整体
来看适度宽松的货币政策仍在发力，但货币政策继续宽松的约束也在加强：从国
内方面看，9 月企业新发放贷款加权平均利率略低于 3%，比上年同期低约 0.2 个
百分点，个人住房新发放贷款加权平均利率约为 3.1%，与上年同期基本持平，9
月底银行间隔夜拆借利率在 1.36%的较低水平，十年期国债利率在 1.68%的较低
水平，人民币对美元汇率增长 10 月初升值突破 6.7；从海外看，市场方面，9 月
底十年美债收益率达到 5.29%的较高水平，同时美联储已于 9 月落地一次加息，
并且纪要显示年内仍将有一次加息。我们认为下一阶段需要关注“适度宽松”的
货币政策可能受外部环境影响，出现国内金融条件“被动收缩”的情况。 
 风险提示：全球通胀二次上行；欧美经济回落速度偏快；国际局势复杂化。

公司投资评级： 
买    入：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内超越基准指数 20%以上； 
增    持：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内超越基准指数 10%-20%； 
中    性：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内相对基准指数变动幅度在-10%-10%之间； 
减    持：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内相对基准指数跌幅在 10%以上； 
未有评级：因无法获取必要的资料或者其他原因，未能给出明确的投资评级。

行业投资评级： 
强于大市：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现强于基准指数； 
中    性：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现基本与基准指数持平； 
弱于大市：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现弱于基准指数； 
未有评级：因无法获取必要的资料或者其他原因，未能给出明确的投资评级。

## Document 205: Cliff Notes_ searching for signal (2026-10-16)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ searching for signal (2026-10-16).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: searching for signal
By Sian Fenner, Ryan Wells & Illiana Jain
13:33 October 02 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia, this week’s main event was the RBA Monetary Policy Board’s (MPB) decision to raise the
cash rate by 25bps to 4.60%. This outcome was universally expected given Governor Bullock had
already communicated that upside risks to inflation had materialised – the conditionality established by

the MPB back in August for considering another rate hike.

### Page 2

Governor Bullock made explicit note of three upside risks. The Middle East conflict was emphasised as
the risk that had “materialised”, given the re-escalation and renewed surge in crude oil and refined
product prices over recent months. The RBA’s liaison warns this could lead to further pass-through of
costs and more persistent inflation pressures. On top of this, domestic capacity pressures and the data
centre/AI investment boom were also noted as upside risks that the MPB remain alert to.

In a video update mid-week, Chief Economist Luci Ellis explains why the bar for a follow-up rate hike in
November is low. We now expect a 25bp increase in November, provided there is no sudden resolution
of the Middle East conflict beforehand, or some other event that significantly lowers the outlook for
energy-related costs.
Subsequently, the August CPI revealed that underlying (trimmed mean) inflation rose 0.2% (3.4%yr),
which was slightly below the market’s expectation but spot on Westpac’s forecast. As usual, the

monthly detail was mixed, but the key drivers of underlying inflation remain centred on housing-related
components, particularly rents and new dwelling purchases. Headline inflation remained volatile, with
the unwind of the fuel excise cut causing another spike in fuel prices. We have maintained our forecast
for a 0.9% lift in trimmed mean inflation in Q3, and with upside risks building for Q4, a follow-up rate
hike in November appears most likely.

Regarding the current growth pulse, household spending surprised with a flat reading in August.
Services spend fell back following recent strength that was driven by major sporting events, but this
was offset by an increase in goods spend, particularly in fuel and electric vehicles. Together with our
latest card data for early September, the consumer spending pulse is shading a touch softer. However,
another solid outcome for business investment could once again be on the cards for Q3. August’s

goods trade data showcased another significant spike in data centre-related equipment imports, up
$4.3bn, similar in scale to Q1. While clearly import-driven, our modelling suggests that the local
economic impact of such investment is likely to be around half of the total investment.
Before moving offshore, a final note on housing. Cotality’s latest update revealed that dwelling prices
fell another 1.1% nationally in September. While Sydney and Melbourne have led the correction to

date, the fact that the other capitals are also reporting price declines clearly indicates that the
correction is deepening and broadening, as higher interest rates collide with the tax policy changes
announced in May’s budget. Sentiment effects indeed remain at play, as evinced by the continued pull-
back in investor credit growth. Westpac anticipates a peak-to-trough decline of around 7%, with tight
on-market supply limiting the extent of the correction. For more, see our latest Housing Pulse.

Offshore, there was plenty of data to dissect.
In the US, inflation eased in August despite ongoing strength in household demand and higher energy
prices. Headline PCE inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.4%yr, while core PCE increased 0.2%mth and
3.0%yr. Encouragingly for the FOMC, the share of PCE components rising by more than 3% over the
past year, a measure frequently cited by Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh, declined to 45% from 49%,
suggesting some easing in broad-based inflation pressures. At the same time, household spending

remained robust, increasing 0.9%mth, equivalent to around 0.6%mth in real terms. That said, personal
income growth was soft, rising 0.2% in the month, signalling households opted to draw down savings.

### Page 3

The ISM manufacturing survey was stronger than the headline suggested. While the index edged down
0.1pt to 54.5, manufacturing has now expanded for nine consecutive months. There were some signs
of capacity constraints emerging, with production softened in the month but backlogs up 4.6pts to 56.4,
suggesting that firms struggled to keep pace with demand. New orders rose 1.6pts to 55.3, primarily
due to domestic demand as new export orders eased, backlogs increased. Firms responded by lifting

employment, 1.5pts to 52.7. Against this backdrop manufacturers saw a renewed acceleration in cost
pressures, with the prices paid index jumping 6.8pts to 77.9. Firms cited higher steel and aluminium
prices, elevated energy costs, rising freight charges and ongoing semiconductor shortages, with every
major industry reporting higher input costs during the month.
The past week’s data will have done nothing to bridge the clear divide across the FOMC, as evident in

this week’s busy Fedspeak. Some participants argued for further rate hikes, citing strong inflation,
diminished supply capacity and policy that is not yet restrictive. Others counselled patience, pointing to
progress on inflation and that September’s hike buys time to assess upcoming data. The one thing
both agree on is that inflation will be the primary determinant of the timing and extent of further rate
hikes. Our view remains that one further hike is likely this cycle to bring policy closer to neutral.

In China, policymakers announced another round of targeted support measures aimed at stabilising
growth and supporting the struggling property sector. The PBOC lowered rates on its Pledged
Supplementary Lending facility by 25bps to 1.5% and expanded quotas for targeted lending programs.
Authorities also introduced subsidies for eligible first-home buyers. The subsidy applies to homes with
a floor area of 120m² or less and a value below CNY1.5mn, with support capped at 1ppt of mortgage

costs, up to CNY10,000 and for a maximum term of five years. The measures complement reforms
announced in August to improve developer financing conditions and support housing completion.
However, weak credit demand and confidence remains a significant constraint, suggesting policy
transmission is still limited.
The September PMI data pointed to a modest improvement in activity, although the details were less
convincing. Manufacturing returned to expansionary territory, rising 0.9pts to 50.1, supported by

stronger production and new orders. Much of the improvement, however, reflected a rebound in prices,
with the raw materials price index rising 7.6pts and producer prices increasing 6.2pts. Labour market
conditions remained weak, with the employment index falling a further 0.6pts and remaining below 50
for almost four years. Conditions in the non-manufacturing sector also improved, with the services PMI
lifting from 49.0 to 50.2, driven largely by stronger construction activity. Input and output prices moved

higher, selling prices returned back above 50, export orders improved to 48.5 and employment
increased 0.5pts, although at 45.9 it remains firmly in contractionary territory. Overall, the surveys
suggest activity is stabilising and policy support is gaining some traction, but weak labour demand,
subdued credit growth and soft domestic demand indicate the recovery remains fragile.
In Japan, the September Tankan survey delivered another constructive signal on the economy and

reinforced the case for further policy normalisation. Business conditions improved by 2pts for large
manufacturers and 5pts more broadly, while profitability indicators strengthened and capital
expenditure intentions remained elevated. Large manufacturers expect fixed investment to increase by
11.6% in FY2026, while large non-manufacturers expect investment growth of 11.2%, pointing to

### Page 4

Related articles
continued confidence in the medium-term outlook.
Capacity indicators improved further across both manufacturing and non-manufacturing sectors, with
measures of excess production capacity becoming less negative and indicating that demand remains
sufficiently strong to keep pressure on existing capacity. Labour shortages also intensified, with
employment conditions becoming more negative across most industries and firm sizes, pointing to a

progressively tighter labour market. Inflation expectations remained anchored around 2%, providing
further evidence that Japan is moving towards a more sustainable inflation environment. Overall, the
survey was consistent with an economy operating close to capacity and supports the view that a
December rate hike remains a realistic possibility.

## Document 296: Oil prices hold firm despite Persian Gulf supply growing

Source PDF: all report pdf/Oil prices hold firm despite Persian Gulf supply growing.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Oil prices remain ﬁrm despite rising supply from the Persian
Gulf. We expect oil prices to trade lower into year-end. More
positive signs of a potential US-Iran deal would give us more
conﬁdence in this view. Meanwhile, European gas looks
vulnerable heading deeper into the heating season
Warren Patterson
Oil prices are holding ﬁrm despite Persian Gulf supply increasing
Persian Gulf oil ﬂows pick-up
There are few signs of de-escalation in the Middle East. If anything,

it appears as though the conﬂict is broadening, with the Houthis in
Yemen increasingly targeting Saudi Arabia. Despite little
improvement in the geopolitical environment, oil ﬂows from the
Persian Gulf have been trending higher with supply from the region
returning to 70-75% of pre-war levels, although there are some
estimates suggesting we could be back to as high as 80-90% of
pre-war ﬂows.

### Page 2

The fact that oil prices are well-supported despite the improvement
in ﬂows from the region appears to reﬂect concerns over how
sustainable this supply is, particularly when considering the
geopolitical backdrop. The market continues to price in a large risk
premium in the absence of a deal between the US and Iran. And to
see this risk premium erode, the market may need the comfort of a
deal. Failing that, time may be enough for oil prices to trend lower,

assuming supplies from the region continue to hold up. For now, we
are holding onto our 4Q26 Brent forecast of $80/bbl and FY27
forecast of $71/bbl. However, given the high level of uncertainty,
this is a low-conviction call, speciﬁcally for 4Q26.
As oil ﬂows from the Persian Gulf have picked up, another
bottleneck has become increasingly noticeable in the oil market.
There is signiﬁcant tightness in the tanker market, which has seen
tanker freight rates surge to record levels. Oil ﬂows from the Persian

Gulf have become increasingly ineﬃcient due to the challenging
environment, which has absorbed more of the global tanker ﬂeet.
And the only way this starts to normalise is if the way oil ﬂows
from the region also starts to normalise.
The other pain point for the oil market remains diesel, which will be
a concern as we head further into the Northern Hemisphere winter.
Disruptions from the Persian Gulf and Russia have tightened the
market, while the risk of export restrictions from the US has kept the

market nervous. The risk of US export restrictions appears to have
subsided, with Europe set to release diesel from strategic reserves.
Furthermore, expensive freight suggests that reﬁned product prices
are likely to remain elevated.
Natural gas vulnerable heading into heating
season
European natural gas prices have traded at their highest level since
December 2022, with supply concerns growing as the region heads
into the heating season. Unfortunately for the natural gas market,

we have not seen a similar recovery in LNG ﬂows from the Persian
Gulf as we have with oil. Therefore, LNG ﬂows from the region
remain down about 80% year-on-year. The market is increasingly

accepting that Persian Gulf LNG could remain largely absent from
global markets for much of the Northern Hemisphere winter. This
means Asia and Europe will continue competing for spot cargoes
throughout the winter, keeping prices well supported and leaving
the market vulnerable to further spikes.
European gas storage remains a concern, at just under 75% full,

well below the ﬁve-year average of 89%. Low storage has seen the
German government instruct state-owned SEFE to buy and store an
additional 8TWh of natural gas by 15 December. The prospect of
essentially forced buying provides further support to the market.
With little sign of an imminent recovery in Persian Gulf LNG supply,
weather will be crucial this winter, and there is growing expectation
that Europe will experience a winter of milder temperatures due to

El Niño, which would provide some relief to the market. However, it
is not a strong relationship, and El Niño does not guarantee milder
temperatures. The North Asia winter will also be important for the
LNG market, as El Niño can also typically mean milder
temperatures in this region.
We now expect TTF to average EUR73/MWh over the 26/27 winter,
up from a previous forecast of EUR65/MWh. The market will likely be
very volatile over this period, given the lower starting point for gas

storage. Prices are then expected to sell oﬀ aggressively from 2Q27
as Persian Gulf supply normalises, and we see a further ramp-up in
US LNG export capacity.

## Document 302: Q3 Job Vacancies_ another step lower (2026-10-16)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Q3 Job Vacancies_ another step lower (2026-10-16).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#labour #labour #employment
Q3 Job Vacancies: another step lower
By Neha Sharma
Economist, Westpac
13:07 October 01 2026 Share
The number of job vacancies fell by 3.0k (–0.9%qtr) between May and August. The decline
was wholly driven by the private sector, where vacancies fell 2.0%qtr. In contrast, public
sector vacancies rose 7.8%qtr.

### Page 2

Job vacancies fell by 3.0k (–0.9%qtr) between May and August, extending the previous quarter’s
decline. The May and February estimates were also revised slightly lower. As a result, vacancies are
now 1.4% below their level a year earlier. The result was at odds with partial indicators, which all
pointed to a rise in the quarter.

The decline was wholly driven by the private sector, where vacancies fell 2.0%qtr to be 1.5%yr lower.
In contrast, public sector vacancies rose 7.8%qtr, marking the strongest quarterly increase in four years
and partially reversing the sharp 7.5%qtr decline recorded in the May quarter. Public sector vacancies
were flat over the year.

Job vacancies have largely moved sideways since early 2025, though a resumption of the broader
downtrend now appears increasingly likely. Labour demand has held up reasonably well, though not
sufficiently to match growth in labour supply, resulting in a gradual easing in labour market conditions
(see here). Today’s result is also consistent with our view that this process will continue into early 2027
as the cumulative effects of high inflation and higher interest rates continue to weigh on activity and
hiring demand.

### Page 3

The vacancy-to-unemployment ratio, a key measure of labour market tightness closely monitored by
the RBA, declined from 0.48 to 0.45. The fall was the sharpest since early 2025, reflecting both a
decline in vacancies and a 6.8%qtr increase in the number of unemployed people over the quarter,
largely owing to the continued strength in labour supply. The ratio remains well above its long-run
average of around 0.2.

Given the strong growth in labour supply, the Beveridge curve provides a useful framework for
assessing recent labour market developments. By comparing vacancies and unemployment as shares
of the labour force, it controls for growth in the overall size of the labour market. Between 2022 and
2024, the labour market moved down the steep portion of the Beveridge curve, with substantial
declines in vacancies occurring alongside only modest increases in unemployment. While labour
market tightness has continued to unwind, Australia remains in this region of the curve, suggesting that
further rebalancing between labour demand and supply can occur through lower vacancies without a
material rise in unemployment.

### Page 4

All statistics mentioned below are not seasonally adjusted and hence may not add up to the seasonally
adjusted statistics referenced above.

By industry, job vacancies declined in eight of the 18 industries over the three months to August. The
largest falls were recorded in wholesale trade (–18.2%qtr), professional, scientific & technical services
(–17.4%qtr), and information media & telecommunications (–16.7%qtr). Consumer-facing sectors were
mixed, with retail trade (24.6%) and arts & recreation services (10.3%) recording strong increases,
while accommodation & food services edged 0.9% lower. Only three industries have seen vacancies
fall below their pre-pandemic levels: administrative & support services (–24.3%qtr), information media
& telecommunications (–9.1%qtr), and financial & insurance services (–7.3%qtr).

Overall market sector vacancies were flat in the quarter, meanwhile the non-market sector saw a 2.9%
drop, owing to declines in education & training (–8.2%qtr) and health care & social assistance (–
4.2%qtr). For detailed insights on industry condition, see Q2 Labour Account and the Westpac
Business Signal.

## Document 321: The hobby economy and the price of fun Consumers are paying more for fun as Millennials and Gen Z reshape how they spend their leisure time. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/The hobby economy and the price of fun Consumers are paying more for fun as Millennials and Gen Z reshape how they spend their leisure time. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-09-01
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13020137     1

Economy 
The hobby economy and the price of fun 
23 September 2026

Key takeaways 
• Consumers are spending more on hobbies. Hobby spending rose 7.9% year-over-year (YoY) in August, but this was more than 
double the pace of transaction growth, suggesting the reemergence of "funflation" in hobbies, according to Bank of America 
card data. 
• Older Millennials are the biggest hobby spenders, according to Bank of America card data, despite having the least amount of 
leisure time of any generation. They also have the highest share of the population with hobby spending, though this likely 
reflects both personal interests and spending on their kids. 
• Gen Z's hobby habits are evolving. While hobby purchases have slowed sharply, younger consumers continue to devote 
significant leisure time to gaming, with over a quarter of Gen Z having some type of online or video game spending, according 
to Bank of America card data.

Spending grows over twice as fast as transactions in August 
Bank of America card data suggests we’re seeing some “funflation” (the rising price of fun) in hobbies – defined here as arts and 
crafts stores, hobby shops and outdoor recreation service providers and retailers that specialize in activities like hiking, camping, 
skiing and scuba diving (see Methodology).  
Using our definition, spending on hobbies grew 7.9% year-over-year (YoY) in August 2026. This was over twice as fast as 
transaction growth, which was up a solid 3.4% YoY (Exhibit 1). This is the opposite of last August, when transactions outpaced 
spending by nearly a percentage point. It’s also a stark contrast from 2023, when spending fell nearly 4% YoY and transactions 
were up almost 1%.  
Exhibit 1: Spending grew at twice the rate of transactions in August 2026 
Spending and transaction growth per household on hobbies (three-month moving average, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Looking across a longer period, we see that spending growth also outpaced that of transactions throughout the pandemic 
(Exhibit 2). Transactions began declining in 2021, with spending following suit in mid-2022. In fact, spending declined faster 
than transactions from 2023 until late 2024, with both improving the following year.

### Page 2

In our view, it’s likely that people splurged on hobbies amid social distancing during the pandemic, then gravitated back toward 
pricier alternatives like travel throughout 2022 and 2023. However, last year there may have been some rotation back to less 
expensive leisure like hobbies as after-tax wage growth slowed considerably (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Still sizzling). 
Bank of America card data suggests there’s been a recent acceleration in hobby spending in the past few months, possibly as 
some consumers balance shift away from travel due to higher prices from rising fuel costs. 
Exhibit 2: Spending growth outpaces transaction growth, while the opposite was true from mid-2022 until the end of 2024 
Spending and transaction growth per household on hobbies (three-month moving average, YoY%) and average spending per transaction growth per household 
on hobbies (three-month moving average, YoY%, right-hand side (rhs))

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Older Millennials claim the hobbies crown, while Gen Z lags behind 
Looking across generations, Bank of America card data suggests that older Millennials spend the most per customer on hobbies, 
followed closely by Baby Boomers and Gen X, while Gen Z spends the least (Exhibit 3).  
Exhibit 3: Older Millennials spend the most on hobbies, followed by Baby Boomers and Gen X 
Average spending per customer by age generation (three-month moving average to August 2026, index Gen Z = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data   
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Older Millennials also boast the largest share of their population with hobby spending (Exhibit 4 and Exhibit 5). In our view, it’s 
likely because this group is most likely to have young children. Which may mean older Millennials not only spend on their own 
hobbies but on their kids’ as well.

The idea that older Millennials are likely spending on themselves and their kids is supported by data from the American Time Use 
Survey (ATUS) conducted by the US Census Bureau. That survey suggests that older Millennials have the least amount of leisure 
time, with only around four hours and fifteen minutes on average per day (Exhibit 6). Meanwhile, the two groups between ages 
“15 and 24” and “55 and 64” have nearly five and a half hours per day, with those 65 and older boasting over seven hours of 
leisure time per day.   
On the other hand, younger Millennials and Gen Z have the lowest average spending, but a higher share of their population with 
hobby outlays. To us, this suggests that these generations are likely gravitating most of their spending toward less expensive 
hobbies like arts, crafts and board games. 
Exhibit 6: Leisure time varies considerably by age range, with those 35 to 44 years old having the lowest amount

some Baby Boomers in the 55 to 64 years old range.

Gen Z consumers slow hobby purchases… 
Additionally, while younger and older Millennials as well as Gen X appear to be ramping up hobby spending, Baby Boomers and 
Gen Z are slowing theirs (Exhibit 7). Notably though, Gen Z’s slowdown in purchases has been stark. Transaction growth was near 
zero in August 2026, a dramatic slowdown compared to the nearly 16% YoY increase the prior year, according to Bank of 
America card data (Exhibit 8).   
Exhibit 7: Millennials and Gen X are ramping up hobby spending, 
while Gen Z and Baby Boomers are winding it down 
Spending per customer on hobbies by age generation (three-month 
moving average, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 8: Gen Z saw a significant slowdown in hobby-related 
transactions 
Purchases per customer on hobbies by age generation (three-month 
moving average, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

This slowdown was driven by a significant decrease in spending on outdoor recreation, offsetting increases in arts and crafts 
retailers and hobby shops. In our view, the prior year’s transactions may have been boosted by purchases of longer-lasting items, 
such as hiking boots, which don’t need to be bought as frequently. It’s also possible that this reflects a shift to more “granny 
core” activities like knitting, sewing, or baking (arts and crafts). 
…and ramp up their gaming spend 
Additionally, some transactions at hobby shops may require less frequent purchases like board games or tabletop role-playing 
games. The social component to these activities can’t be overlooked as a factor. In fact, over a quarter of Gen Z said they feel a 
sense of belonging to a streaming or gaming community, according to a Bank of America survey (Exhibit 9). This is almost two 
and half times as high as younger Millennials and over eight times the rate of Baby Boomers.  
Exhibit 9: Gen Z is over twice as likely to feel a sense of community or belonging to a streaming or gaming community than any other generation 
The share of those that answered “gaming” to the question “Outside of your family, in which places or groups do you currently feel a sense of community or 
belonging?” by age generation (see Methodology for time period, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data

Also, Gen Z spends anywhere between 20 and 25% of their leisure time playing games or using a computer, according to the 
ATUS (Exhibit 10). This is nearly twice the rate of Millennials and a threefold increase compared to young adults (ages 25 to 34 
years old) from two decades ago. And while a portion of this shift certainly reflects an increase in social media use (read more in 
Wellness, part 5: Lost in the scroll), it likely also reflects an increase in time spent playing video games.

Exhibit 10: People 25 to 34 years old spend nearly one out of every five minutes of their leisure time playing games or using a computer, over three 
times the rate seen in 2003 when nearly one in every seventeen leisure minutes was spent this way 
Share of leisure time spent playing games or using a computer (annual, %)

Source: US Census Bureau American Time Use Survey 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Gen Z has seen their video game spending increase by around 20% YoY in August, after an increase of over 30% YoY in the year 
prior, according to Bank of America payments data (Exhibit 11). And this is while boasting the highest average spending as well, 
with over three and a half times more video game spending than Baby Boomers and almost twice as much as Gen X (Exhibit 12).  
Bank of America payments data also suggests that over a quarter of Gen Z have video game spending, over four times the rate 
of Baby Boomers (Exhibit 13). Younger Millennials have the next highest rate at over 23%, with about one in five older 
Millennials having video game spending.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Hobby spending includes spending across all payment vehicles at arts and crafts stores, hobby shops, ski resorts, scuba retailers and rentals, 
adventure activities, educational toys, outdoor retailers and some toy stores. Not included in this analysis are some hobbies like golf or travel, 
due to further analysis in other pieces. Also not included are more digital forms of entertainment like video games, though this category is 
included as a separate analysis.   
Includes a reference to Bank of America’s Navigator Survey’s question “Outside of your family, in which places or groups do you currently feel a 
sense of community or belonging? (Select all that apply).”  The survey was conducted between January 16 and 22, 2026 and included 2,041 
participants.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. Our Gen Z analysis only includes 
spending by people that are 18 years or older and likely reflects the trends from the members of this generation who are living on their own.  
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

## Document 335: Top of Mind - Assessing a Less Transparent Fed

Source PDF: all report pdf/Top of Mind - Assessing a Less Transparent Fed.pdf

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh is steering the Fed into a less transparent era. So, is that
a positive or negative development, and what could it mean for markets and the
economy? We speak with Former Fed governors Donald Kohn and Stephen Miran
as well as GS’ Jan Hatzius who agree that rigid “Odyssean” forward guidance that
commits policymakers to a pre-set path risks policy mistakes and is therefore rarely
appropriate. But they somewhat disagree about the benefits of great er transparency

around the Fed’s reaction function. Hatzius and Kohn argue that such transparency
helps markets better anticipate and price in policy changes, accelerating monetary
policy transmission. But Miran believes that the line between committing to a path
and being transparent is clearer in theory than in practice. And while GS economists
and strategists find that reduc ed transparency leads to increased market volatility,
the question is whether this just increases the market’s noise or also improves the

market’s signal. Hatzius argues largely for the former and Miran for the latter.
Jan Hatzius, Chief Economist and Head of Global Investment Research, Goldman Sachs
“Markets price what they think the Fed will do, not what they think the Fed should do. That will remain true even if the Fed
obscures its reaction function… market guesses about the Fed’s next moves will simply become worse, leading to more
volatility... and importantly, volatility that serves no constructive economic purpose.”

Donald Kohn, former Vice Chairman of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, Robert V . Roosa
Chair in International Economics and Senior Fellow, Brookings Institution
“I sympathize with Chairman Warsh to some extent. [But] the real problem with Warsh’s approach is his reluctance to articulate
how the FOMC sees the economy evolving… explaining the Fed’s thinking about the economic outlook is essential in helping
markets act as a stabilizing force and is a core component of accountability.”
